\hypertarget{about-the-book}{%
\chapter{About the book}\label{about-the-book}}

This book contains all the documentation for the paediatric concussion
subtyping project of my PhD. Each chapter is made of quarto (.qmd) files
in the github repository
\href{https://github.com/psvelayudhan/research}{psvelayudhan/research}.
R scripts are interwoven with the documentation by being
\href{https://bookdown.org/yihui/rmarkdown-cookbook/child-document.html}{child-imported}\footnote{Not
  the same as sourcing R scripts. The Rmarkdown files are structured to
  be executed sequentially, and each script reads in the data it
  requires and writes out the data for subsequent analyses to reduce
  their inter-dependence.} from R markdown files in the
\href{https://github.com/psvelayudhan/subtyping-mtbi}{psvelayudhan/subtyping-mtbi}
repository.

Because the scripts actually read and write to our lab's carbon share,
most code chunks are set to not execute on render using the
\texttt{eval=FALSE} flag. To reproduce any of the analyses in this book,
rendering the book is not enough; you must step through the linked
source code yourself. Occasional chunks that do run are self-contained
and are expected to simply read and display output generated by the
``serious chunks'' that were manually run previously.

\part{Home}

\part{Introduction}

\hypertarget{mild-traumatic-brain-injury}{%
\chapter{Mild traumatic brain
injury}\label{mild-traumatic-brain-injury}}

This chapter is largely taken from my MSc thesis.

\hypertarget{definition-and-epidemiology}{%
\section{Definition and
Epidemiology}\label{definition-and-epidemiology}}

Traumatic brain injuries (TBIs) refer to any injury in which an external
force applied to the head results in structural injury or physiological
brain disruption\textsuperscript{1}. These injuries are typically
categorized by severity as being mild, moderate, or severe. The precise
definitions for TBI and its subcategories of severity vary across
sources based on the injury's specific causes and
symptoms\textsuperscript{2}.

The ambiguity of defining TBI is particularly prominent for mild
traumatic brain injuries (mTBIs)\textsuperscript{3}. One common
definition that is used to categorize a TBI as mild by sources such as
the World Health Organization (WHO)'s Collaborating Centre for
Neurotrauma Task Force\textsuperscript{4} and the United States
Department of Veterans Affairs\textsuperscript{5} is that the injury
must reveal no abnormalities on traditional methods of structural brain
imaging, no more than 30 minutes of lost consciousness following the
impact, no more than 24 hours of altered mental state or confusion, and
a score above 13 on the 15-point Glasgow Coma Scale
(GCS)\textsuperscript{1}. The GCS is a clinical tool that examines a
patient's eye-opening, verbal, and motor responses to commands and
physical stimulation to determine their precise level of consciousness
along the spectrum of completely non-responsive to completely
responsive\textsuperscript{6}.

The term mTBI is also commonly used interchangeably with the term
concussion\textsuperscript{1,7}, however, some organizations believe the
term concussion should only be used to describe a specific form of
(typically sports-related) mTBI, or to describe a distinct injury that
shares some overlap in causes and outcomes as mTBI\textsuperscript{8}.
Others also believe the ambiguity associated with the term concussion is
sufficient reason to not use it at all\textsuperscript{9}.

A recent article published in JAMA insights outlined the state of
clinical post-mTBI care and management as of 2019\textsuperscript{10}.
Potentially as a result of the complexity of the condition, there are
currently no therapeutic interventions that are known to mitigate the
consequences of mTBI; the resolution of symptoms is the primary metric
used to determine the pace at which an mTBI patient may resume tasks of
varying cognitive and physical demand\textsuperscript{10,11}. Stricter
caution is used in cases where there is higher risk of sustaining a
subsequent concussion or injury, such as for athletes, military
personnel, and the elderly.

The high prevalence of mTBIs, their potentially serious and persistent
outcomes, and their lack of effective treatments and therapies position
them as a leading global health concern and an important priority for
research.

\hypertarget{outcomes-and-heterogeneity}{%
\section{Outcomes and heterogeneity}\label{outcomes-and-heterogeneity}}

Though mTBIs are the least harmful form of TBI, they are thought to make
up between 80-90\% of all TBIs\textsuperscript{1} and afflict
approximately 600 per 100,000 people every year\textsuperscript{7}. A
study by Boak et al.~reported that upwards of 15\% of children in
Ontario sustain an mTBI each year\textsuperscript{12}. While the
majority of mTBIs result in transient, self-limiting symptoms which
resolve within 7-10 days\textsuperscript{8}, approximately 10-20\% of
all mTBIs can lead to a wide range of neurological, cognitive, and
behavioural symptoms including depression\textsuperscript{13},
anxiety\textsuperscript{14}, headaches\textsuperscript{15},
dizziness\textsuperscript{16}, fatigue, increased
irritability\textsuperscript{14}, difficulties in
concentration\textsuperscript{17}, impairments in memory, abnormal
sleeping patterns\textsuperscript{18}, emotional
volatility\textsuperscript{19}, stress intolerance\textsuperscript{20},
decreased reaction times\textsuperscript{21}, and visual tracking
ability\textsuperscript{21}, which may take months or years to resolve,
or may never resolve at all\textsuperscript{1,17,21}. Patients with mTBI
have also been identified as having higher incidence rates of conditions
such as dementia\textsuperscript{7} and chronic traumatic
encephalopathy\textsuperscript{22}. In approximately 25\% of
cases\textsuperscript{23}, symptoms following mTBI persist for several
months or years after the initial trauma.

Understanding the heterogeneity of mTBI outcomes is a major challenge in
the field. Our ability to predict what types of symptoms a patient will
have, how long those symptoms may persist for, and what treatments will
be most beneficial to them is still limited. Previous studies have made
progress on this topic by identifying several biological, social, and
psychological factors that explain at least some of the heterogeneity of
mTBI outcome severity, duration, and type.

Some of those previous studies attempted to further parse the
heterogeneity of mTBI by using those factors to define subtypes of mTBI
patients (discussed more in (Section~\ref{sec-subtypingmtbi}).

\hypertarget{sex-differences}{%
\subsection{Sex differences}\label{sex-differences}}

One notable component of the heterogeneity of mTBI is the differential
outcomes experienced by males and females. A search of the PubMed
database for review papers in the last 10 years that have examined how
sex influences clinical mTBI outcomes (search: ``sex'' and (``mTBI'' or
``mild traumatic brain injury'')) identified\textsuperscript{8} reviews
which found that females generally had worse outcomes following
mTBI,\textsuperscript{18,24--28}, 1 review which found that males
generally had worse outcomes\textsuperscript{29}, two reviews reporting
mixed outcomes\textsuperscript{30,31}, and two reviews reporting no
conclusive differences between the sexes\textsuperscript{13,32}.

Though clinical studies tend to suggest that females experience poorer
outcomes than males, a recent systematic review by Gupte et
al.\textsuperscript{33} examining sex differences following TBI of all
severities found that in general, the opposite was true for animal
studies. This review also found that the proportion of studies which
reported a particular type of sex difference seemed to vary considerably
with impact severity; among the 12 mTBI studies examined, 25\% (n = 4)
reported that females had worse outcomes and 17\% (n = 2) reported that
males had worse outcomes, but among the 31 moderate-severe TBI studies
examined, only 10\% (n = 3) reported that females had worse outcomes and
55\% (n = 17) reported that males had worse outcomes. Whether this
pattern was the result of chance due to the small number of mTBI studies
or a reflection of a biological interaction effect of impact severity
with sex remains to be determined.

Though there are several studies which suggest that sex can influence
outcomes of mTBI, it is not yet clear how or when these differences in
outcomes occur. Including sex as a parameter in replicable experimental
mTBI studies and examining how it interacts with other study factors
will be a necessary component of future work to fully understand the
pathophysiology and possible treatments of mTBI.

Biological, psychological, and social variables have been found to
associate with mTBI outcome severity, type, and duration.

\hypertarget{mechanisms-of-damage}{%
\section{Mechanisms of damage}\label{mechanisms-of-damage}}

Pathological sequelae of TBI are commonly split into primary and
secondary damage phases\textsuperscript{34}. The primary damage phase
refers to the immediate effects of the mechanical disruption of the
brain, while the secondary damage phase encompasses longer lasting
cellular and molecular mechanisms that are initiated by the consequences
of the primary damage\textsuperscript{35}.

\hypertarget{primary-biomechanical-mechanisms-of-damage}{%
\subsection{Primary biomechanical mechanisms of
damage}\label{primary-biomechanical-mechanisms-of-damage}}

Though the mechanisms by which trauma to the head can result in injury
to the brain are not completely known, novel advances in our
understanding of the contributing biomechanical, cellular, and molecular
processes are continuously being made.

Compared to TBIs of higher severities, the major effects of the primary
damage phase of mTBIs are less defined by direct trauma to the brain and
more defined by the straining and shearing forces generated by the rapid
changes in the brain's rotational and linear
acceleration\textsuperscript{36}. These extreme changes in acceleration
generate intracranial pressure gradients that permeate through the
brain\textsuperscript{1} and cause irreversible damage to critical brain
structures\textsuperscript{1}.

\hypertarget{secondary-cellular-and-molecular-mechanisms-of-damage}{%
\subsection{Secondary cellular and molecular mechanisms of
damage}\label{secondary-cellular-and-molecular-mechanisms-of-damage}}

The initial mechanical forces of an mTBI can trigger secondary cellular
and molecular processes that perpetuate brain degeneration or hinder
brain recovery for years after the trauma. This secondary damage is
composed of several complex mechanisms whose interactions are the
subject of current investigations, and include oxidative
stress\textsuperscript{34}, mitochondrial
disruption\textsuperscript{37}, inflammation\textsuperscript{38}, axon
degeneration\textsuperscript{39}, and cellular
apoptosis\textsuperscript{40,41}.

TBIs can cause damage through the transient disruption of cell
membranes, which can create imbalances in intra- and extra-cellular
concentrations of ions and neurotransmitters\textsuperscript{42}.
Acutely after an injury, neuronal presynaptic terminals can aberrantly
release glutamate, resulting in disrupted neuronal communication across
the brain. The excessive extracellular concentrations of glutamate are
exacerbated by the widespread dysfunction of glutamate transporters in
the acute phase of the injury\textsuperscript{43}. Potassium can also be
released by neurons in a manner proportionate to the severity of the
trauma\textsuperscript{44}, which can further exacerbate the disruptions
in inter-neuronal signaling.

TBIs can also result in abnormal buildups of intracellular calcium ions,
which can in turn trigger excessive mitochondrial calcium uptake and
subsequent impaired mitochondrial function\textsuperscript{42}.

Another consequence of TBI is the so-called `post-TBI energy
crisis'\textsuperscript{45}, which describes the significant drop in
brain glucose uptake following injury. The mechanisms by which glucose
uptake is inhibited following TBI is not known but could be attributed
to any combination of impaired glucose transporter function at the blood
brain barrier, impaired levels of cerebral blood flow, and cellular
impairments in metabolic processing.

Overproduction of free radicals and oxidative stress as a result of
intracellular calcium accumulation\textsuperscript{34} is another
component by which TBIs are known to damage the
brain\textsuperscript{42}. While reactive oxygen species (ROS) are a
normal product of the brain's metabolic functions, TBI can result in ROS
levels that are too high for homeostatic ROS scavenging molecules to
mitigate\textsuperscript{46}. The overabundance of ROS can go on to
exacerbate brain damage by altering cell membrane
permeability\textsuperscript{47}, disrupting DNA
repair\textsuperscript{48}, and causing mitochondrial
dysfunction\textsuperscript{49} among other harmful consequences.

The activation of glial cells is also recognized to be a major mechanism
by which inflammation, degeneration, and repair are regulated following
mTBI. At rest, astrocytes play important roles in establishing
homeostatic conditions in the central nervous system, supporting
neuronal function, and maintaining the integrity of the blood-brain
barrier\textsuperscript{50}. After a mild traumatic brain injury,
astrocytes adjust their morphology and gene expression signature (a
reaction known as astrogliosis\textsuperscript{51}), proliferate and
migrate towards damaged structures\textsuperscript{52}, buffer ionic
imbalances through cellular pumps and channels, and secrete both
damaging and reparative cytokines and chemokines\textsuperscript{53}. In
more severe injury contexts, astrocytes are also capable of forming
scars that protect healthy tissue from the inflammatory molecules at the
sites of damaged structures\textsuperscript{54}.

Microglia, the resident immune cells of the central nervous system, also
have a complex role in the pathophysiology of mTBI. Under homeostatic
conditions, microglia constantly survey their surrounding area for signs
of trauma or pathogenic invasion using highly motile processes, which,
upon detection, can induce a heterogeneous set of contextually specific
and morphologically, transcriptionally, and functionally activated
microglial states\textsuperscript{55}. Similar to astrocytes, microglia
have both damaging and reparative functions when activated following
mTBI. Reparative functions of microglia include phagocytosing cellular
debris and producing scavenger receptors, anti-inflammatory cytokines,
and growth factors, while damaging functions include producing
pro-inflammatory cytokines and excessively phagocytosing tissues that
are healthy or capable of being repaired\textsuperscript{53,56,57}.

The complete interplay and ordering of the previously outlined
mechanisms are complex and likely dependent on the specific combination
of patient and injury factors. However, in addition to the primary
mechanical forces described in the previous subsection, many of these
secondary cellular and molecular processes play a significant role in
mediating a major correlate of post-mTBI impairment: damage or
disruption to the brain's white matter.

\hypertarget{white-matter-vulnerability-to-mtbi}{%
\subsection{White matter vulnerability to
mTBI}\label{white-matter-vulnerability-to-mtbi}}

White matter regions of the brain, which are structures composed mainly
of myelinated axons that primarily function to relay information between
different brain regions, are known to be highly susceptible to both
primary mechanical and secondary molecular damage processes following
mTBI\textsuperscript{58--60}.

Although all cells and structures in the brain are affected to some
degree by the mechanical forces associated with mTBI, white matter
structures are particularly vulnerable to primary damage due to their
high composition of long neuronal axons\textsuperscript{58}. Rather than
the overt degeneration of white matter that may be more commonly seen in
severe forms of TBI, mTBI often results in axon damage and axon-myelin
pathology\textsuperscript{59}. Diffuse axonal injury (DAI), the most
common form of axonal injury associated with mild injuries, is initiated
by the mechanical forces of the injury and characterized by widespread
patterns of damaged axons interspersed among intact ones, typically in
midline white matter tracts such as the corpus callosum and internal
capsules\textsuperscript{39,59}. In cases of mTBI, axonal damage has
been observed to occur without surrounding vascular damage, suggesting
that axons are substantially more susceptible to these injuries than
blood vessels\textsuperscript{59}.

Both the mechanical action of the impact as well as the byproducts of
structures that were damaged by that action can go on to initiate
secondary cellular and molecular processes of brain
damage\textsuperscript{40}.

Though the precise mechanisms by which secondary molecular and cellular
damage and repair processes affect white matter following mTBI are not
fully understood, some contributing processes have been identified. For
mild brain injuries, those mechanical forces may cause axonal
cytoskeleton disruption or imbalances of intra-axonal ion concentrations
that primarily lead to swelling and sometimes secondary axonal
disconnection. Another secondary mechanism by which white matter damage
can occur is by degeneration or loss of oligodendrocyte lineage cells,
which can subsequently lead to the disruption or loss of
myelin\textsuperscript{59}. This disruption may be initiated by a
variety of mechanisms after head trauma, including glutamate
excitotoxicity\textsuperscript{61}, oxidative
stress\textsuperscript{62}, and inflammatory consequences of activated
astrocytes\textsuperscript{53,63} and
microglia\textsuperscript{53,64,65}.

The general progression of white matter damage following mTBI is thought
to typically begin with DAI from the primary impact causing unmyelinated
axon fibers to degenerate and myelinated axon fibers to become damaged
at the nodes of Ranvier\textsuperscript{59}. Swelling and atrophy of
distal axon segments following initial damage then further disrupts
surrounding myelin. Secondary processes can then initiate demyelination
of intact axons and additional axonal damage, again beginning from the
nodes of Ranvier\textsuperscript{59,60}. Axons that are sufficiently
damaged enough may become irreversibly disconnected.

Myelin sheaths, the insulating layer of axons, are part of
oligodendrocytes. Myelin plays a major role in axon vulnerability to
damage: unmyelinated axons are especially susceptible to traumatic
axonal injury. As well, the axon initial segment, an unmyelinated region
of the beginning of an axon, is often the initial site of axon cleavage
(axotomy) following traumatic axonal injury. Myelin debris is also
enough to trigger the activation of microglia and infiltrating
macrophages\textsuperscript{66}, which may play a significant role in
propagating secondary damage following mTBI. Myelin debris has also been
shown to inhibit the differentiation of oligodendrocyte precursor cells,
which is a necessary process for myelin
regeneration\textsuperscript{67,68}.

Importantly, damage to white matter structures following mTBI has been
strongly associated with the cognitive, neurological, and behavioural
impairments that can follow the injury\textsuperscript{59,64,69}, making
them an important candidate target for the development of mTBI therapies
and biomarkers.

\hypertarget{sec-neuroimaging}{%
\section{Neuroimaging}\label{sec-neuroimaging}}

The subtle white matter damage that follows mTBI is most commonly
detected (in both clinical and preclinical contexts) through
neuroimaging and histological analysis\textsuperscript{59}. In the
context of living subjects (which is the relevant context for this
project), the histological approach to detecting white matter damage is
not really applicable. The usage of neuroimaging (particularly MRI) for
mTBI diagonosis and prognosis is described in detail in a later chapter
in this book (Section~\ref{sec-neuroimaging}).

To better detect white matter damage in patients of mTBI, advanced MRI
techniques, such as diffusion tensor imaging (DTI), are being
increasingly used46. Because white matter has a more fibrous structure
than gray matter, the diffusion of water within it is more directional.
DTI scans measure the degree of directionality, or fractional anisotropy
(FA), of water molecules over the entire brain in 3D units known as
voxels. In a study that collected DTI scans of 76 adults that had
received an mTBI 1 to 2 weeks prior, the reduction of FA was found to be
a predictive measure of long-term outcome57. Other studies have reported
both increases58 and decreases59, 60 in post-TBI FA, suggesting that
even the type of structural white matter changes that follow a TBI are
complex and likely depend on multiple factors. Within white matter
tracts that are complex and/or long-range, the accuracy of DTI in
detecting abnormalities is still limited46.

\hypertarget{cluster-analysis}{%
\chapter{Cluster analysis}\label{cluster-analysis}}

One of the most effective and commonly used approaches for identifying
subtypes of items in any context is cluster analysis. In this chapter, I
outline some basics of cluster analysis and how clustering has been
applied to mild traumatic brain injury.

\hypertarget{the-basics-of-clustering}{%
\section{The basics of clustering}\label{the-basics-of-clustering}}

\hypertarget{distance}{%
\subsection{Distance}\label{distance}}

Identifying appropriate distance measures can be one of the most
challenging parts of a cluster analysis pipeline.

Normalization prior to similarity matrix generation is only important
for multidimensional input matrices.

In the SNF analyses ahead I use the following distance measures: *
standard normalization + euclidean for multidimensional continuous
matrices (like subcortical volumes of many regions)

\hypertarget{commonly-clustering-techniques}{%
\section{Commonly clustering
techniques}\label{commonly-clustering-techniques}}

\hypertarget{sec-mtbi-subtyping-literature}{%
\section{Clustering applied to
mTBI}\label{sec-mtbi-subtyping-literature}}

\hypertarget{similarity-network-fusion}{%
\section{Similarity network fusion}\label{similarity-network-fusion}}

Similarity network fusion (SNF), developed by Wang et al.~in
2014\textsuperscript{70}, is an example of such a technique which works
by first constructing similarity pairwise similarity matrices of all
observations for each data type and then merging those matrices into a
single network in a way that captures the full spectrum of the
underlying data.

Advances in technology have facilitated the collection of massive and
diverse datasets. To harness the full potential of these datasets for
pattern detection, prediction, and decision-making, techniques to
effectively integrate diverse types of data are necessary.

The most basic form of data-integration is to simply normalize and
concatenate all available data types prior to analysis. This approach is
known to reduce the signal-to-noise ratio of each individual data
type\textsuperscript{70}, which is a serious concern in the context of
biological data that is already very noisy to begin with. The authors of
SNF also note that attempting to enhance signal by only including a
pre-selected subset of data measures (features) can result in biased
analyses.

Analysis of each data type prior to combination can be difficult when
the results of the independent analyses don't agree with each
other\textsuperscript{70}. Circumventing this issue with consensus
clustering (clustering based on the agreement of multiple different
clustering algorithms) can result in the loss of valuable complementary
information (i.e., the information that is not shared across data
types), which is somewhat antithetical to the purpose of integrating
diverse data-types in the first place.

The core procedure associated with SNF analyses involves the following
steps:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Construct a similarity matrix for each pair of samples for each
  available data type
\item
  Fuse the similarity matrices using an iterative, nonlinear method
  (SNF)
\end{enumerate}

User-defined parameters of the pipeline include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  The data types to include
\item
  The similarity measure to use for each data type
\item
  Hyperparameters related to the fusion step (though the original SNF
  paper demonstrates strong robustness
\end{itemize}

The topic of feature selection (determining which variables to include)
is not given much attention in the original description of the SNF
pipeline. Though feature selection is known to be important in
traditional cluster analysis due to the sensitivity of the algorithms to
high-dimensionality,

\hypertarget{spectral-clustering}{%
\section{Spectral clustering}\label{spectral-clustering}}

\hypertarget{cluster-analysis-evaluation}{%
\section{Cluster analysis
evaluation}\label{cluster-analysis-evaluation}}

\hypertarget{cluster-tendency}{%
\subsection{Cluster tendency}\label{cluster-tendency}}

Will not examine the Hopkins statistic for cluster tendency - ``The
relatively high value for H in this single-cluster case is due to a
comparison of the structure of this data set so uniformly distributed
random numbers, Le., data intended to be completely unstructured. With
unstructured data as a comparison, virtually any structure in the test
data would lead to rejection of the null hypothesis whether the data are
clustered or not''\textsuperscript{71}. However even such a variation of
the Hopkins statistic would not be so interesting, and would likely be
expected - demographic data is random noise.

Whether or not the clusters are randomly distributed. This is
challenging in the context of biomedical data - we do not expect the
clusters to be randomly distributed by default (the demographic
composition of any given country is far from random). In the context of
mTBI, there are many facets of heterogeneity that would be interesting
to examine:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Heterogeneity of children with mTBI vs.~those without

  \begin{itemize}
  \item
    \textbf{Identifying the risk-of-sustaining-mtbi groups}: of all the
    children without a history of mTBI at baseline, is there a
    difference between the clustering of those that will develop an mTBI
    later on in the study and those who will not?
  \item
    \textbf{Identifying the types of mTBI patients}: how does the
    heterogeneity of those who have mTBIs differ from the heterogeneity
    of those who don't have an mTBI? (Contrasted with the previous
    bullet point, this will help us learn more about the clusters that
    are formed BECAUSE of the mTBI and not because of being at a higher
    risk of mTBI)

    \begin{itemize}
    \item
      \textbf{Identifying the types of mTBI outcomes:}

      \begin{itemize}
      \item
        purely examining the clusters of patients using PPCS as the only
        input variable
      \item
        can contrast mTBI PPCS vs.~non-mTBI PPCS (though in that case,
        it's not quite a PPCS)
      \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

  f\left(k\right) = \binom{n}{k} p^k\left(1-p\right)^{n-k}
  (\#eq:binom)
\end{equation}

\hypertarget{yeates-et-al.-2019}{%
\subsubsection{Yeates et al., 2019}\label{yeates-et-al.-2019}}

\textbf{Derivation and initial validation of clinical phenotypes of
children presenting with concussion acutely in the Emergency Department:
Latent class analysis of a multicentre, prospective cohort,
observational study}

\hypertarget{narrative}{%
\paragraph{Narrative}\label{narrative}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Participant selection:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Participants were 8-18 yo children from 5P
  \item
    Excluded children for variety of reasons including missing data
  \item
    Included children for mTBI
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Data collection:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Data collection on variety of tests at acute (\textless48 hrs), 4
    weeks, 12 weeks
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Data pre-processing: Collected data split into 3 domains (pre-injury
  history, clinical presentation, acute parent-rated symptoms)
\item
  Unsupervised learning: LCA conducted to identify clusters of each
  domain separately
\item
  Within clusterings cluster characterization:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Described clusters by proportions of low/medium/high values of the
    input variables
  \item
    Membership in each cluster was associated with PPCS risk at
    follow-up timepoints
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Across clusterings characterization:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Membership in the different clusters were significantly associated
    using Cramer's V
  \item
    Created `phenotypes' based on common patterns of membership across
    the three LCAs and examined risk of PPCS for combinations of
    8-12yo/13-18yo and male/female

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      note that a theoretical maximum of 4\emph{4}7 (permutations of
      membership across the LCAs) `phenotypes' exist in this framework
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Supervised learning:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    multivariate regression using age, sex, and group membership was
    effective at predicting PPCS
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{rough-extraction}{%
\paragraph{Rough extraction}\label{rough-extraction}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  n: 2323 8-18 year old children from the 5P cohort
\item
  Age on injury: 8-18 years old
\item
  Age on data collection: 8-18 years old
\item
  Time between injury and data collection: \textless{} 48 hours - 12
  weeks
\item
  Concussion definition: ``Brain injury stemming from a direct blow to
  the head, face, neck, or elsewhere on the body with force transmitted
  to the head, and resulting in 1 or more symptoms in 1 or more of the
  following domains: somatic, cognitive, emotional or behavioral, or
  sleep''
\item
  Exclusion criteria:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Glasgow Coma Scale score \textless{} 14
  \item
    structural abnormality on neuroimaging (if performed)
  \item
    neurosurgical intervention
  \item
    intubation or intensive care unit admission
  \item
    multisystem injury requiring hospitalization
  \item
    procedural sedation
  \item
    severe preexisting neurological developmental delay
  \item
    intoxication
  \item
    absence of trauma as primary event
  \item
    previously enrolled in the same study
  \item
    insurmountable language barrier
  \item
    inability to follow-up by telephone or email
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Clustering method:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    algorithm: latent class analysis
  \item
    number of clusters: `multiple relevant statistics, theoretical
    plausibility, and interpretability of solutions with consecutively
    increasing number of groups'
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Inputs:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Acute Concussion Evaluation (ACE; completed within 48 hrs)
  \item
    Post-Concussion Symptom Inventory (PCSI; completed within 48 hrs and
    followed-up at 4-weeks and 12-weeks)
  \item
    Standardized Assessment of Concussion (SAC; completed within 48 hrs)
  \item
    Balance Error Scoring System (BESS; completed within 48 hrs)
  \item
    Child-Sport Concussion Assessent Tool-3rd Edition (Child-SCAT3;
    completed within 48 hrs)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Clustering 1:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    premorbid history
  \item
    inputs:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      previous concussion
    \item
      prior concussion symptom duration \textgreater{} 1 week
    \item
      migraine
    \item
      learning disability
    \item
      anxiety
    \item
      depression
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    clusters:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      cluster 1 (78\%):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        low probability of any pre-injury risk factor
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 2 (13\%):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of previous concussion with symptoms
        \textgreater{} 1 week
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 3 (7\%):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of pre-injury anxiety
      \item
        modest probability of pre-injury migraine
      \item
        modest probability of learning problems
      \item
        modest probability of depression
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 4 (2\%):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of previous concussion with persistent symptoms
      \item
        high probability of pre-injury anxiety
      \item
        moderate probabilities of other pre-injury factors
      \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    outcomes:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      membership significantly associated with PPCS risk at 4-weeks and
      12-weeks
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Clustering 2:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    clinical presentation on examination
  \item
    inputs:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      loc
    \item
      dazed and stunned
    \item
      answers slowly
    \item
      forgets recent information
    \item
      SAC orientation score \textless4*
    \item
      SAC normalized total score \textless0.11*
    \item
      BESS tandem stance \#\# of errors \textgreater=4 or physically
      unable to do test
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    clusters:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      cluster 1 (50):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        low probability of any clinical findings on examination
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 2 (23):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        moderate to high probability of being dazed
      \item
        moderate to high probability of answering questions slowly
      \item
        moderate to high probability of being forgetful
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 3 (17):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        moderate to high probability of being dazed
      \item
        moderate to high probability of answering questions slowly
      \item
        moderate to high probability of being forgetful
      \item
        elevated probabilities of deficits on objective tests of
        cognition and balance
      \item
        moderate probability of LOC
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 4 (11):

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of deficits on objective tests of cognition and
        balance
      \item
        low probabilities of impaired mental status
      \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    outcomes:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      membership significantly associated with PPCS risk at 4-weeks
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Clustering 3:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    acute parent-rated symptoms
  \item
    inputs

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      headache
    \item
      nausea
    \item
      balance
    \item
      dizziness
    \item
      clumsy
    \item
      vision problems
    \item
      sensitivity to light
    \item
      sensitivity to noise
    \item
      drowsy
    \item
      fatigure
    \item
      irritability
    \item
      mental fog
    \item
      difficulty concentrating
    \item
      difficulty remembering
    \item
      confused
    \item
      answers more slowly
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    clusters:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      cluster 1:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of all symptoms
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 2:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of headache
      \item
        high probability of dizziness
      \item
        high probability of drowsiness
      \item
        high probability of fatigue
      \item
        high probability of mental fogginess
      \item
        high probability of poor concentration
      \item
        high probability of problems remembering
      \item
        high probability of thinking slowly
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 3:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of headache
      \item
        high probability of nausea
      \item
        high probability of drowsiness
      \item
        high probability of fatigue
      \item
        no cognitive impairments of cluster 2
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 4:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probabilities of physical and fatigue symptoms
      \item
        low probabilities of cognitive symptoms
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 5:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of headache
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 6:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of headache
      \item
        high probability of nausea
      \item
        high probability of drowsiness
      \item
        high probability of fatigue
      \item
        high probability of balance problems
      \item
        high probability of dizziness
      \end{itemize}
    \item
      cluster 7:

      \begin{itemize}
      \tightlist
      \item
        high probability of headache
      \item
        high probability of dizziness
      \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    outcomes:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      membership significantly associated with PPCS risk at 4-weeks and
      12-weeks
    \end{itemize}
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Primary outcome:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    PPCS (defined as persistence over 4-weeks of at least 3 symptoms
    relative to baseline; evaluated at 4-weeks and 12-weeks)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Additional notes:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    sample collected from original 5P study
  \item
    children needed \textgreater= 80\% of all variables for inclusion
  \item
    clustering was not used to predict specific symptoms, but rather the
    presence or absence of PPCS
  \item
    sex and age were included \emph{a priori} as additional predictors
    for supervised learning due to their known prognostic significance
  \item
    \textbf{may need to request the study data to better understand the

\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{project-aims}{%
\chapter{Project aims}\label{project-aims}}

I hypothesize that:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Distinct subtypes of subjects in a chronic-stage following paediatric
  mTBI, defined by variables related to the patient, their environment,
  and their injury, exist.
\item
  Those subtypes will be associated with distinct profiles of persistent
  post-concussive symptoms.
\item
  The heterogeneity among children that have sustained an mTBI is
  largely driven by variables that influence or were influenced by the
  injury, suggesting that the identified subtypes would not match the
  subtypes identified in the general paediatric population
\item
  Identified subtypes (and subject membership among those subtypes) will
  be relatively invariant over time
\end{enumerate}

If these hypotheses are supported, identifying such subtypes may
significantly improve our understanding of risk factors, prognoses, and
potential mechanistic targets for therapeutic intervention. As well,
variables that define these subtypes may inform data-driven patient
stratification for upcoming clinical trials.

Though the proposed work for this project is primarily exploratory in
nature, I intend on testing these hypotheses over the course of
completing the project aims listed below. Predictions that follow the
above hypotheses are described under the relevant objective
descriptions.

\part{The PNC dataset}

\hypertarget{the-pnc-pilot}{%
\chapter{The PNC pilot}\label{the-pnc-pilot}}

Dr.~Wheeler has previously conducted some pilot SNF analyses within the
Philadelphia Neurodevelopmental Cohort (PNC) dataset.

A main qualitative finding from this pilot was that subtype \#3 had
reduced performance on the line orientation test, higher likelihood of
experiencing LOC, and decreased functional connectivity between the
brainstem and occipital cortex.

The code in those previous analyses (with small adjustments) are shown
below. Only the code outputs relevant to the qualitiative finding above
are included.

\NormalTok{clinical }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ clinical[, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# recode factors as numbers 1=yes, 0=no}
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(clinical)}
\NormalTok{clinical}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_LOC }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(clinical}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_LOC }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"yes"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{clinical}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_AMN }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(clinical}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_AMN }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"yes"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{clinical}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_HEADACHE }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(clinical}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_HEADACHE }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"yes"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{clinical\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(clinical)}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.character}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{unlist}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ]))}
\NormalTok{clinical\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ clinical\_t[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ]}
\FunctionTok{rownames}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{()}

\CommentTok{\# remove ppl with no data or NAs}
\NormalTok{clinical\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.data.frame}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t)}
\NormalTok{clinical\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ clinical\_t[}\FunctionTok{colSums}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{clinical\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ clinical\_t[}\FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t,}
    \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t) }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{any}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t)))]}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# 6: Demographic Info for TBI participants}
\NormalTok{demographics }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read.csv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/tbi\_pnc\_dem.csv"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{check.names =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{demographics }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ demographics[, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}

\NormalTok{demographics\_datadic }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(demographics)}

\CommentTok{\# recode factors as numbers:}
\DocumentationTok{\#\# Sex: 1=male, 2=female, 3=unknown}
\DocumentationTok{\#\# Race 1=EA=1, 2=AA=2, 3=other}
\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(demographics)}
\NormalTok{demographics}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Sex }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}
    \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(demographics}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Sex }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"M"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
        \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(demographics}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{Sex }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"F"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{demographics\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(demographics)}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.character}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{unlist}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ]))}
\NormalTok{demographics\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ demographics\_t[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ]}
\FunctionTok{rownames}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{()}

\CommentTok{\# remove ppl with no data or NAs}
\NormalTok{demographics\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.data.frame}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t)}
\NormalTok{demographics\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ demographics\_t[}\FunctionTok{colSums}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{demographics\_t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ demographics\_t[}\FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t,}
    \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t) }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{any}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t)))]}


\CommentTok{\# Checking for same participants across datatypes}

\CommentTok{\# Create vectors with the column names (which are always the subject IDs)}
\NormalTok{colnames\_rsfc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(rsfc\_data\_wb)}
\NormalTok{colnames\_clinical }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t)}
\NormalTok{colnames\_demographics }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t)}

\CommentTok{\# \textquotesingle{}common\_subs\textquotesingle{} contains only the IDs found in all datatypes)}
\NormalTok{common\_subs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{Reduce}\NormalTok{(intersect,}
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(colnames\_rsfc, colnames\_clinical, colnames\_demographics))}

\CommentTok{\# Subset each dataframes to only have information from common\_subs}
\NormalTok{rsfc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ rsfc\_data\_wb[, }\FunctionTok{which}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(rsfc\_data\_wb) }\SpecialCharTok{\%in\%} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(common\_subs))]}
\NormalTok{clinical }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ clinical\_t[, }\FunctionTok{which}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(clinical\_t) }\SpecialCharTok{\%in\%} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(common\_subs))]}
\NormalTok{demographics }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}
\NormalTok{    demographics\_t[, }\FunctionTok{which}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{names}\NormalTok{(demographics\_t) }\SpecialCharTok{\%in\%} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(common\_subs))]}

\CommentTok{\# Check for matchine number of participants across datatypes}
\FunctionTok{dim}\NormalTok{(rsfc)}
\FunctionTok{dim}\NormalTok{(clinical)}
\FunctionTok{dim}\NormalTok{(demographics)}

\CommentTok{\# Check the sample names to see if they are in the same order in all 3 datasets}
\CommentTok{\# compare IDs for rsfc and clinical}
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(rsfc) }\SpecialCharTok{==} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clinical)) }\SpecialCharTok{==} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(rsfc))}
\CommentTok{\# compare IDs for tract clinical and demographic}
\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clinical) }\SpecialCharTok{==}
    \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(demographics)) }\SpecialCharTok{==}
    \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clinical))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\hypertarget{running-snf}{%
\section{Running SNF}\label{running-snf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# Run SNF using PNC data {-} TBI participants only}

\CommentTok{\# PART 1: SNF SETUP}

\CommentTok{\# load libraries}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(SNFtool)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}

\CommentTok{\# Perform the standard recommended normalisation}
\NormalTok{rsfc.norm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{standardNormalization}\NormalTok{(rsfc)}
\NormalTok{clinical.norm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{standardNormalization}\NormalTok{(clinical)}
\NormalTok{demographics.norm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{standardNormalization}\NormalTok{(demographics)}

\CommentTok{\# For the subsequent analysis we need to transpose the data}
\NormalTok{rsfc.norm.tr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(rsfc.norm)}
\NormalTok{clinical.norm.tr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(clinical.norm)}
\NormalTok{demographics.norm.tr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(demographics.norm)}

\CommentTok{\# Create silarity matrices for each data frame.}
\CommentTok{\# Hyperparameters:}
\CommentTok{\# 1) the number of neighbours, K (normally between 20 {-} 30)}
\CommentTok{\# 2) the hyperparameter alpha, which is normally between 0.3 and 0.8}
\CommentTok{\# 3) the number of iterations, usually between 10 and 50}

\CommentTok{\#Before creating the affinity matrices, we need to first create distance matrices}
\NormalTok{rsfc.dist }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{dist2}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(rsfc.norm.tr),}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(rsfc.norm.tr))}
\NormalTok{clinical.dist }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{dist2}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(clinical.norm.tr),}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(clinical.norm.tr))}
\NormalTok{demographics.dist }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{dist2}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(demographics.norm.tr),}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(demographics.norm.tr))}

\CommentTok{\#Now create the similarity matrices (graphs) using the distance matrices and the K, alpha and T parameters}
\CommentTok{\# First, set all the parameters:}
\NormalTok{K }\OtherTok{=} \DecValTok{10} \CommentTok{\# number of neighbors, must be greater than 1. usually (10\textasciitilde{}30), I have used k = 10, as there are 85 subjects and it looks from Laura stefanik\textquotesingle{}s article that k = number of subjets/10}
\NormalTok{alpha }\OtherTok{=} \FloatTok{0.5} \CommentTok{\# hyperparameter, usually (0.3\textasciitilde{}0.8)}
\NormalTok{T }\OtherTok{=} \DecValTok{50} \CommentTok{\# number of Iterations, usually (10\textasciitilde{}50)}

\CommentTok{\# Construct similarity graphs (can be considered to be fully connected networks)}
\NormalTok{rsfc.sim }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{affinityMatrix}\NormalTok{(rsfc.dist, K, alpha)}
\NormalTok{clinical.sim }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{affinityMatrix}\NormalTok{(clinical.dist, K, alpha)}
\NormalTok{demographics.sim }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{affinityMatrix}\NormalTok{(demographics.dist, K, alpha)}

\CommentTok{\# PART 2: SNF RUN}
\CommentTok{\#SNF with rsFC + clinical + demographics }
\CommentTok{\# first create a list of the 3 similarity "networks"}
\NormalTok{sim.list }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(rsfc.sim, clinical.sim, demographics.sim)}

\CommentTok{\# Construct the fused network. This requires the previously defined K (no. neighbours) and T (no. iterations) parameters.}
\NormalTok{fused.sim }\OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{SNF}\NormalTok{(sim.list, K, T)}

\CommentTok{\# set the row and column names of the fused network to the sample ids}
\FunctionTok{dimnames}\NormalTok{(fused.sim) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(common\_subs)}

\CommentTok{\#estimate the number of clusters for fused network}
\CommentTok{\#this command estimates number of clusters {-} note to add grid search for optimal number of clusters}
\NormalTok{clusters.est }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{estimateNumberOfClustersGivenGraph}\NormalTok{(fused.sim)}

\CommentTok{\# spectral clustering}
\CommentTok{\# clusters.est as 2(first best) and 3(second best)}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2} \OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{spectralClustering}\NormalTok{(fused.sim,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3} \OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{spectralClustering}\NormalTok{(fused.sim,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# add subject IDs to clustering.fused}
\NormalTok{common\_subs}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{as.data.frame}\NormalTok{(common\_subs)}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(common\_subs)[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"SUBJID"}

\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{as.data.frame}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(common\_subs,clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"cluster.2"}

\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{as.data.frame}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(common\_subs,clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\NormalTok{)[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"cluster.3"}

\CommentTok{\#output a csv with SUBJID and cluster number}
\CommentTok{\#write.csv(clustering.fused.2,}
\CommentTok{\#    file = "data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/clusters\_fused\_2.csv")}
\CommentTok{\#write.csv(clustering.fused.3,}
\CommentTok{\#    file = "data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/clusters\_fused\_3.csv")}

\CommentTok{\#calculates NMI for rsfc conn and output csv}
\CommentTok{\#NMIScores.rsfc \textless{}{-}rankFeaturesByNMI(list(rsfc.norm.tr),fused.sim)}

\CommentTok{\#write.csv(NMIScores.rsfc, file = "data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/nmi\_rsfc.csv")}

\CommentTok{\#calculates NMI for clinical}
\CommentTok{\#NMIScores.c \textless{}{-}rankFeaturesByNMI(list(clinical.norm.tr),fused.sim)}

\CommentTok{\#write.csv(NMIScores.c, file = "data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/nmi\_clin.csv")}

\CommentTok{\#calculates NMI for demographic}
\CommentTok{\#NMIScores.d \textless{}{-}rankFeaturesByNMI(list(demographics.norm.tr),fused.sim)}

\CommentTok{\#write.csv(NMIScores.d, file = "data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/nmi\_dem.csv")}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\#\#\# make variable names file}
\NormalTok{rownames.rsfc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(rsfc)}
\NormalTok{rownames.clinical }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clinical)}
\NormalTok{rownames.demographics }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(demographics)}

\NormalTok{var }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(rownames.rsfc, rownames.clinical, rownames.demographics)}

\CommentTok{\# write.csv(var, file = "data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/variables.csv")}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\hypertarget{comparing-clusters}{%
\section{Comparing clusters}\label{comparing-clusters}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}
\CommentTok{\#library(MASS)}


\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{read.csv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/clusters\_fused\_2.csv"}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{read.csv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/clusters\_fused\_3.csv"}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, clustering.fused}\FloatTok{.3}\NormalTok{[,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{])}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused)[}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"cluster.3"}

\CommentTok{\# Bring in outcomes .csv }

\NormalTok{outcomes}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{read.csv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{here}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"data/pnc/pilot{-}snf/tbi\_pnc\_outcomes.csv"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{fill =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{header =}\NormalTok{ T)}

\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{merge}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, outcomes, }\AttributeTok{by=}\StringTok{"SUBJID"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{all.x=}\NormalTok{T)}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused[}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}X.x\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)]}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\ConstantTok{NULL}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused[}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}X.y\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)]}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\ConstantTok{NULL}


\DocumentationTok{\#\# 2 cluster solution {-} Demographics}
\CommentTok{\# number of participants in each cluster}
\NormalTok{clustering.fused}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\FunctionTok{as.factor}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# wrat in each cluster}
\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for wrat}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(WRAT\_CR\_STD }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}
\CommentTok{\# breakdown of each cluster}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{WRAT\_CR\_STD)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{WRAT\_CR\_STD)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{WRAT\_CR\_STD)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{WRAT\_CR\_STD)}

\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for GAF {-} Children\textquotesingle{}s Global Assessment Scale {-} Current}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(GAF001.y }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}
\CommentTok{\# GAF breakdown of each cluster}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF001.y)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF001.y)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF001.y)}

\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for GAF {-} Children\textquotesingle{}s Global Assessment Scale {-} Most Severe Past}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(GAF002.y }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}
\CommentTok{\# GAF breakdown of each cluster}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF002.y)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF002.y)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF002.y)}

\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for GAF {-} Children\textquotesingle{}s Global Assessment Scale {-} Lifetime}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(GAF003.y }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}
\CommentTok{\# GAF breakdown of each cluster}

\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{GAF003.y)}

\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for internalizing problems}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(internalizing }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}

\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for externalizing problems}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(externalizing }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}
\CommentTok{\# breakdown of each cluster}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{externalizing)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{externalizing)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{externalizing)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{externalizing)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{externalizing)}

\CommentTok{\# compare clusters for anxiety problems}
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{aov}\NormalTok{(anxiety }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{data=}\NormalTok{clustering.fused))}
\CommentTok{\# breakdown of each cluster}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{anxiety)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{anxiety)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{anxiety)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{anxiety)}
\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{subset}\NormalTok{(clustering.fused, cluster}\FloatTok{.2}\SpecialCharTok{==}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{anxiety)}

This is the original plot comparing brainstem-occipital rsfc across
clusters. The figure does not plot one data point per subject, but
rather one data point per edge (?) averaged across all subjects within
the cluster.

\includegraphics[width=0.5\textwidth,height=\textheight]{pnc/../figures/pnc/pilot-snf/3_cluster_bs_oct_rsfc.png}

When the values are plotted on a per-subject basis (calculations found
later in this chapter), we get the following graph:

\includegraphics[width=0.5\textwidth,height=\textheight]{pnc/../figures/pnc/pilot-snf/3_cluster_bs_oct_rsfc_per_subject.png}

No significant associations appear between any pair of LOC, line
orientation test performance, and brainstem-occipital cortex functional
connectivity. Now checking just within cluster 3:

\NormalTok{yloc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ association\_df2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{bs\_oct\_rsfc[association\_df2}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{TBI\_LOC }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"yes"}\NormalTok{]}
\FunctionTok{t.test}\NormalTok{(noloc, yloc)}
\CommentTok{\# p{-}value: 0.4896}

    \FunctionTok{geom\_boxplot}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{geom\_jitter}\NormalTok{() }\SpecialCharTok{+}
    \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{text =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
